451. WILLIAM ROLLISTON was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling house of Nathaniel Dawson, about the hour of twelve in the night, of the 16th of September, and burglariously stealing therein, a mahogany desk, value 2l. 12s. 6d. three pair of plated salt holders, value 1l. three razor cases, value 16s. twelve rasors, value 5s. three pair of steel scissars, value 4s. his goods.
NATHANIEL DAWSON sworn.
I live in Huggin-lane, Wood-street, Cheapside. On Thursday morning I was informed that my house, No. 8, in the Strand, was broke open; two partners of us has the house together.
Q. Does your partner live in that house? - No. neither one of us.
Q. Don't you keep servants there? - None, it is an empty house, only there are goods in the shop.
Q. How long is it since any body has resided or slept there? - Three weeks or a month.
Q. Did you leave beds there? - No.
Q. You keep it merely as a sale shop? - Nothing more. Thursday morning I was alarmed that the house was broke open, and I went up there immediately, and I was informed that a man was in St. Martin's watch-house, and I went to see him, and the constable of the night told me that was the man, and told me to be at Bow-street by eleven o'clock.
Q. When you went up to your house on this alarm, did you find it had been broke open? - I did; I missed a writing desk, three pair of plated salt holders, &c. from the shop.
Q. Have you recovered any property you missed? - None.
Q. Why do you impute it to the prisoner? - I know nothing at all about it.
Q. You have a partner in this house inthe strand; is he not a partner in your business? - Yes.
Q. Then half the part of the articles in the house is his as well as your's? - They are.
Not GUILTY.
Tried by the first Middlesex Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
[trialAccount t17950916-82]
452. JOSEPH BROWN was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dewlling house of John Fonblanque, about the hour of ten in the night, of the 29th of July, and burglariously stealing therein, a mahogany knife case, value 10s. twenty-four table knives and twenty-four table forks, value 1l. twelve desert knives, value 4s. twelve desert forks, value 4s. a silver sugar ladle, value 5s. ten child's linen shirts, value 10s. and four pair of child's cotton stockings, value 2s. the goods of the said John Fonblanque .
JOHN ELLIS sworn.
Q. Do you live in Mr. Fonblanque's family? - I do, as his clerk, No. 17, Charlotte-street, Bloomsbury.
Q. What is his Christian name? -John. On Wednesday, the 29th of July, about half past nine, I went from the New square, Lincoln's-inn-fields, to Charlotte-street, Bloomsbury, home to sleep; on going up the steps I attempted to ring the bell for admission, I believe it was then ten minutes before ten; on going up the steps of the street door I was going to ring the bell, but on turning my head I see the door was on a jar; I then did not ring, but pushed the door open with my hand; at the foot of the stairs in the passage, I see the prisoner at the bar and another man, each with a knife case in their arms, on seeing them three I asked them what they wanted? there was a lamp in the passage, they were under that lamp; on asking them what they wanted the prisoner at the bar, who was nearest me, answered, it is only a mistake; I then said, what do you mean by a mistake, you have been robbing the house, they said they had not, both of them, the prisoner said again it is only a mistake; I asked them what business they had with the knife cases in their hands? they made me no answer; the prisoner attempted to pass me, being first; I stopped himm, and laid hold of him by the collar, and told him be should not go till he could give me a better account of himself; a struggle then ensued, and the prisoner attempting to escape made his escape down the steps of the door; I had still hold of him; his companion was a distance behind at the foot of the steps; finding he could not get from me, he threw down this knife case on the pavement; he then threw back his arms as if he would have struck me, but he did not; on his throwing down the knife case his companion passed, and ran away, on his running away I called out steop thief! and watch! several times; I still kept hold of the prisoner; on my calling out watch, a watchman and a number of people came to my assistance; I delivered charge of the prisoner to the watchman, and at my feet lay this knife case, which I took up and took it into the house, and I found it contained twelve knives and eleven forks, the knife case was broke to piece, I took and set it down, and knocked violently against the door, and two of our maid servants came up; on my first going in to the door I see no marks of violence whatever, the door was in its usual state, and the key hanging on the arm of the chain, where it ususally does; I went up stairs for a pistol, and I searched the rooms, and found nothing material; the same night I missed, besides the two knife cases, atrunk; I don;t know what the trunk contained; I went to the watch-house and the prisoner was there, and there was produced to me a spoon which I believe to be the property of Mr. Fonblanque.
Q. Can you identify any of the property? - The knife case I can; the knife case was on the side board in the front parlour, close to the passage, and the trunk was in the passage up the stairs; I see the knife case before I went out, about six o'clock in the evening.
Q. Are you a judge of the value of these things? - Not particularly I am not.
Q. Have you any doubt that that is the knife case of Mr. Fonblanque? - I have not the least doubt.
Mr. Knowlys. How long had you lost sight of the person, whoever he was, before you got the watch-house? - About ten minutes; from the time that I delivered him to the watchman, and the time that I see him at the watch-house, might be about ten minutes.
Q. You gave charge of the same person to a watchman? - I did.
Q. Now I would ask you whether you did not pitch on a person that did not turn out to be the prisoner at the bar, when you got to the watch-house? - No, I did not, I am quite sure of it; the prisoner at the bar was among a number of others, and he was behind them, and I looked about till I see him.
Q. Did you never pitch on another man first? - No.
Court. Are any of these servant girls of the house here? - They are not.
Q. Then how do you suppose these men got in; can you give any account of it? - I cannot.
VALENTINE RUMLEY sworn.
I am the watch house-keeper of St. Giles's.
Q. Look at that young man, Ellis, did he deliver to you a prisoner, at any time? - Yes, on Wednesday, the 29th of July, past ten o'clock, the prisoner at the bar, on suspicion of breaking open the house, and taking knives and forks; I searched him, and found a silver strainer in his pocket, and two or three keys belonging to his father.
Q. You have kept the strainer ever since? - Yes.
Q. To Ellis. Does that belong to Mr. Fonblanque? - I believe it does.
Q. You missed such a one? - I did, from a plate in the closet, in the same parlour where the knives were taken from, it was supposed to be left on the side table where the knife case was.
Q. When had you last seen it? -Some days before, in the closet of the parlour, the crest is exactly the same, a family crest, a helmet.
Prisoner. My defence I shall refer to my counsel, and rest on the mercy of you and the gentlemen of the jury.
The prisoner called eight witnesses who gave him a very good character, said he had been an apprentice to a taylor, and lately had worked with his father in the bricklayering line.
GUILTY.
Of stealing to the value of 39s. and not breaking and entering.
Judgement respited .
Tried by the first Middlesex Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
[trialAccount t17950916-83]
453. PETER LANGLEY was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 21st of August, half a guinea, the money of Joseph Thorn.
JOSEPH THORN sworn.
I am a journeyman carpenter; on the 21ft of August I wrote a letter, between the hours of twelve and one, I sealed thehalf guinea in the letter. I sealed it with the wax before I sealed the letter; the half guinea was under the cover and the wax, between the cover and the letter.
Q. What sort of a seal did you seal it with? - It was a seal that I borrowed of the landlord of the house.
Q. What was on the seal? - I cannot recollect.
Q. What was the landlord's name? -Holt, he keeps the sign of the Bunch of Grapes, in Well-street, Oxford-street; I live just by, and I used that public house to have my beer.
Q. Who was present when you sealed this letter, and put in the half guinea? - Rebecca Hyatt, the servant of this house, She brought me a candle of seal it, she see it. Then I put my letter into my pocket, and went out till between the hour of four and five in the afternoon, then I came to the Bunch of Grapes again; I asked if Mr. Holt would be so kind as to give the postman that letter at six o'clock, when he usually came by; in the mean time the bell man came; I said, Mr. Holt, be so kind as to step out and give the man that letter; and I stood by the side of him and see him give the man the letter; the prisoner is the bellman; he asked him if he would take this letter? he said he would; he asked him if it would go safe? he said he would enter it in the book when he came to Lombard-street, and it would go safe; Holt asked him what it would come to; he paid him eleven-pence for the postage; and he doubled it once double in his hand, and he carried it away in his hand, he did not put it into the bag with the rest, and in the course of the week I had no answer to the letter that I sent, and I sent another letter, and had another letter describing that that first letter was not received; when I found the letter had not come to hand, I went to the inland office to examine the books, and they ordered me to come the next day at eleven o'clock; that was the Monday week after the letter was sent; the person that took it in was not there then; I then the next day, the person examined the books, and there was no such thing entered in the books; I went back to Well-street, and at five o'clock I met the postman, and I asked him what I was to do to get my money; that Tuesday, the same day as I was at LOmbard-street.
Q. Where did you see him? - At the Bunch of Grapes door, where I put in the letter; I asked him whether I was to lose it or no? he said he knew nothing about it, he put it into the office; I asked him if that was all the recompence I was to have for it, I said if that was all I would go and get a warrant, and then I went and got a warrant.
Q. Did you ever see this bell-man in your life before? - Never before, he took the half guinea, he was quite a stranger to me.
Q. You never heard his name mentioned? - Never to the best of my knowledge, not I did not know his name when I got the warrant.
Mr. Knowlys. The prisoner told you that it would be entered in a book? - He said he would enter it in a book when he came to Lombard-street.
Q. I don't know whether you know the regulation of the office, that if a man does neglect to enter it in the book, and any thing is lost, he is obliged to pay for it? - I don't know, I don't want to prosecute the man, not hurt him, and she justice ordered us to make it up; I only want my money.
Q. He did offer you the half guinea by the justice's recommendation? - At that time he did.
Q. And who was the justice? - Mr. Scott.
Q. And I believe besides that you claimed some extra money for expenses?- Yes, I claimed my expences; I did not want to he any thing out of my pocket; it is very hard for me to lose my money.
Q. I should be very glad to know what the extra expences were? - The loss of my time, two days going to Lombard-street, and the expences of the warrant, and serving of the warrant; a guinea was asked in all to pay every thing.
Q. Then you asked nine shillings and seven-pence besides the half guinea and the postage, and he knowing that nine shillings and seven-pence would save him from a prosecution at this bar, refused to pay it? - I suppose that is the case.
Q. Had you the civility when you took him up, to acquaint the post office, that a man, a servant of theirs was taken up, to ask them what they would do for their servant? - I did not.
Q. To whom was the letter directed? - To Mary Anthill.
RICHARD HOLT sworn.
I am a publican. Thorn gave me a letter, and begged of me to give it the bellman, which I did, and paid him eleven-pence, as a double letter; I told him there was half a guinea in it, I asked him if it would go safe. It was the 21st of August, the evening bell.
REBECCA HYATT sworn.
Q. Are you a servant at the Grapes, public house? - Yes.
Q. Did you ever see Joseph Thorn put any thing in a letter at any time? - I did, half a guinea, on Friday, the 21st of August, between four and five in the afternoon, he put the half guinea down, and put the sealing wax on it, and sealed it.
Q. Where did he get the seal? - I don't know.
Q. To Holt. Where did he get the seal? - I don't know.
Q. Do you know whether it was your seal that he used? - I don't know that it was.
MARY ANTHILL sworn.
I live in Bath; I received one letter to inform me that there was a half guinea sent from London, and I went to the post office, and the gentleman of the post office informed me that there never was such a letter came there.
Q. When did you receive your letter? - On Friday, a week after I sent to Mr. Thorn, to desire him to send me a trifle of money.
Q. Have you got that letter that came to you? - No, I burnt it at Bath.
Q. Who was that letter from? - From Mr. Thorn.
Q. What did he acquaint you with? - That he sent me half a guinea before in a letter, that he sent it directly as he received my letter, by the return of post.
Q. Did he say the half guinea was inclosed in the letter? - Yes, he did.
Q. It is very odd you should burn that letter; did you shew it to the post office? - Yes, I shewed it at the post office twice, at Bath.
Q. Was this letter sealed with a wafer? - No, with sealing wax, I never noticed the seal.
Q. How came you to receive this letter? - Because I had not sent any answer to the first letter that he sent.
Q. To Prosecutor. Did you desire an immediate answer to your first letter? - I did, I have the letter that I received from her, that she had not received the half guinea.
Q. What date is that letter? - The 30th of August.
Q. What did she say in that letter? - That she received neither letter nor money.
Prisoner. I believe it is a malicious prosecution; had I paid the prosecutor what he wished to demand of me it would not have been, but he would not name and sum, and there was a runner there that wished to be paid, and when I found that he wanted to make a property of me, I said that I had rather be committed to prison, and trust the event.
HUGH FERGUSON sworn.
Q. I believe you are one of the inspectors of the letter carriers in the post office. Will you be so kind as to tell us what is the practice of the office, with respect to letters paid money with? - The general rule is, when a man takes a letter that money is paid with, to have it entered in the money book.
Q. In the hurry of business, does it, or does it not, frequently happen that that is omitted? - It does; the post master's permanment order is, that those that neglect that duty, shall pay the loss, besides a suspension.
Q. How long has this man been in the employ of this office? - Two years.
Q. During that time, what has been his character and behaviour in the office, good or bad? - To the best of my opinion as honest a man as ever was.
Q. How many hands does a letter pass through between here and Bath? - I cannot say, a great number.
Q. Was he in the service of the office when apprehended? Supposing him acquitted, would he be taken into the office again? - I have not a doubt of it.
Q. With regard to his settling of his accounts at the post office, has that man been regular and punctual, or not? -Always regular and punctual.
Court. Do they turn them out of their office if they refuse to pay the money? - There never has been such an instance, but I am very confident they would be dismissed if they were guilty of such a thing as that.
Q. This letter was put into the post office on Friday, the 21st of August; do you remember whether at that time there was any robbery of the mail, or any loss of letters complained of? - Not that I know of.
Q. Supposing it not being entered in the money book, might it not go to Bath? - Yes, but a letter may go to Salisbury or Bristol and we never hear of it again.
Q. But in general do not they come back to the office when they go wrong? - Yes, but sometimes they do not.
- SPARKES sworn.
Q. Are you employed by the post office? - Yes, a deputy inspector; the prisoner was letter carrier, I have known him about two years, he made his payments always regular.
Q. You have been in court when Mr. Ferguson was examined, he has spoken to the practice of this entering in the money book? - That is the practice.
Q. Does it frequently happen that that practice is omitted? - We have frequently instances of it, from the hurry of business.
Q. Supposing the letter to be lost, the party can be no gainer by it? - He is liable to make it good, or forefeit his place.
Q. What do you think the place of a letter carrier is worth to a man? - Fourteen or sixteen shillings a week.
Court. When there is any money in it, it is entered in the money book? - Yes, and they pay as a double letter.
JOHN WILSON sworn.
I am letter carrier in the general post office, I have known the prisoner since he was in the post office, about two years; with respect to the regularity of his payments and accounts, every body had a good opinion of him.
Q. I believe you know that in the hurry of business, it has frequently happened that persons omit to put down a letter in the money book? - Yes, it has occurred to me.
Q. I believe you can tell us that a letter in going from here to Bath, must pass through a vast number of hands, and may likewise be lost by accident? - It may.
Q. Now I would ask you, supposing for instance it had occurred to you not to have a letter put down in the money book, and you was obliged to pay the money, would you, or would you not, have thought it disagreeable to have paid more than the money? - I would have returned the money contained in the letter, and the postage; I never knew any thing further.
Q. Would you, or would you not, have thought it a suspicious thing to have returned more than the usual money? - I should.
Q. You hold the post of a letter carrier, it is esteemed generally a valuable post? Is it a post that interest is made to obtain? would you part with it for a hundred pounds? - No.
Q. Would you part with it for half a guinea? - I would not.
Q. Nor nine shillings and seven-pence? - I would not.
Court. You know it is capital offence to steal money out of the letters? - Yes.
Q. You know that men has been hung for it? - Yes.
The prisoner called two witnesses who gave him a good character.
- CABAIN sworn.
On the morning of the 22d of August, the prisoner informed me that he had omitted to put such a letter in the evening before, that he had received it, I believe, in Oxford-road; that he had received the postage of that letter, and was informed that it contained money, that in consequence of that he brought that letter to the Post office, with the other letters for which he had received postage, that he had paid for the postage of the letter in the proper office, but that in consequence of the celerity with which business was obliged to be done with us, he had neglected to have it entered in the usual money book.
Q. If he had paid the postage of it, it must have found its way into the Post office at the same time? - It must. some days afterwards, I cannot tell the distance of time, about six or seven days, he again came to me, and told me that that letter had not found its situation to the place where it was directed, he was apprehensive it must be lost: I told him if that was the case, as he had neglected to have it entered in the proper book, he would be answerable for the amount that the letter contained. The man acquiesced in that demand.
Q. Do not these things frequently happen? - It is unpleasant to say that it does frequently happen.
Q. How many hands do you think the letter must pass through between this and Bath? - At least five or six; I don't think it can pass through a great many more.
Jury. It does not pass through any more hands after the bag is sealed up on the road to Bath? - It does not; but at Bath it goes through several hands.
Mr. Knowlys. What character has he borne in the office? - Always borne a very fair character, nor have I observed any impropriety in his conduct whatever.
Court. Where is this money book kept? - It is kept in the paid letter office at the Post office.
Q. When the bellman brings his letters, who separates the money letters from the others? - He should do it himself.
Q. Then when they are so separated, what does he do with them? - He gives them to the officer attending in the paidletter office, informing him that they contain money.
HENRY AUSTIN sworn.
I am president of the inland office.
Q. Of course you know the prisoner at the bar? - Yes. On the 1st of September he came to me, very much agitated, and said, that he had been applied to by a gentleman of whom he had received a letter containing money, who had informed him that the letter had not got to hand. I asked him whether he had received charge with it at the time, and whether he had seen it entered at the time he delivered it in? He said he had forgot to do that; I told him as that was the case he must pay the man whatever it was; which he promised to do.
Q. The prisoner was continued in the Post office as letter carrier, notwithstanding this thing had transpired, and he was the person that let you know that the fact had really happened? - He has been in the office about two years; I never had the least doubt of him.
Q. Would he be taken into the service of the Post office again? - Yes, he would.
Q. They, I understand, take up this defence? - They do.
Q. It is at their desire that you attend? - It is.
Court. When there is a money letter, and when there is not, do not they separate the letters? - They do.
Q. And they always distinguish that to the person that takes them at the window? - Yes.
Q. How many of these money letters do you think may be brought by a bellman at a time? - A man may bring three or four in the evening, and he may not bring one in the course of a week.
Not GUILTY.
Tried by the first Middlesex Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
[trialAccount t17950916-84]
454. WILLIAM HENRY WILCOX was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 4th of July, twenty-three loaves of bread, value 18s. the goods of Henry Freame.(The case opened by Mr. Knowlys.)
HENRY FREAME sworn.
Q. I believe you are a baker in Northumberland street, in the Strand? - Yes.
Q. Was the prisoner, Wilcox, in your service at the time this matter took place? - He was a twelvemonth from the 16th of April last. On the 4th of July, in consequence of missing bread before, I counted my bread that was left in my kitchen, there were twenty-six, twelve, and seven quartern loaves; on that day, I being unwell, had requested Mr. Lashbrooke, a neighbour, to be at the top of the street, in case he should resist. About ten o'clock in the morning he had been down to Westminster, serving all there; he then came in and put two loaves in his pail, and out he went.
Q. Did you see him? - I did not. He went out with the pail to fetch yeast; in consequence of which I looked into the kitchen and missed two loaves. I immediately followed him; I came up with him about the middle of the street, or hardly so high, it may be twenty or thirty yards from the door; when I came up to him, I took hold of him, and I believe I said, you scoundrel, where are you going with that? I perceived two loaves in the pail; I do not recollect that he made any reply whatever; but requested me to let him walk without being held. I told him he need not be ashamed of me if he was not of his action. Mr. Lashbrooke came up within three yards when I laid hold of him. I took him to the watch house; I immediately left him there to go home with my fresh bread, which was laying in a very awkward state. I first see himagain over at the Bear, in Bow-street; he then confessed.
Q. Did you promise him any thing if he would confess? - NO.
Q. Did you make any threats? - Neither to my knowledge. I asked him if he was not ashamed to serve me in the manner he had done? He intreated me for mercy; he begged I would forgive him, on account of his children; that was nearly the expression he made use of; I told him he had no thought of me and my children, or else he would not have done an act of the kind; I said, I suppose let you tell the story, you would say this is the first time; he said, no, I don't say it is the first, it is the second; I said, you know I have put my hand in my pocket and lent you money, and when you have offered to pay me half of it, I said, you had better leave it and pay me when you can. He then went over to Bow-street, and was committed.
Mr. Moore. You had a good character with this man? - I had, or else he should not have been taken into my house.
Court. Has he a large family? - I never knew he had but two children; but that day he told me he had a third.
JOHN LASHROOKE sworn.
I live in Hungerford-market. On the morning of this day I was about a hundred yards from Mr. Freame's door, about ten o'clock in the morning. I see the prisoner come out of Mr. Freame's house with a pail at his back; he walked some little distance from the house; I see Mr. Freame come out of the house after him, which was a signal to me to stop him; I then crossed over the way then and came within six or seven yards of him, and by that time Mr. Freame had come up with him; I stopped him, Mr. Freame was behind him and I was before him. There were two quarters loaves in the pail, when taken off his back at the watch-house.
Q. Did he say any thing? - I did not hear. Mr. Freame said, he had heard of people carrying iniquity in their bosom, but thou carriest it at thy back.
Q. To Prosecutor. What is the value of these two loaves? - Ten-pence halfpenny a quartern loaf, and nearly all the money out of my pocket.
Prisoner. I leave it to my counsel. My master entirely left the business to his daughter; he entirely looked after the bakehouse. I, that morning, had taken out a great deal of bread; I always went down into the kitchen for what bread I wanted; I was going to serve customers with this bread, they lay in my way that I was going for the yeast; it was a considerable wet morning, and I put these loaves in the pail, and my master came out after me. I always used to make it my practice to take the bread out of the kitchen, while there was any bread in the kitchen.
Court to Prosecutor. Was he at liberty to go down into the kitchen and take what bread he pleased? - No, he brought it into the shop and put it on the counter when the shop was emptied, and it was counted.
Prisoner. Were you ever there to do it yourself? - I was there, or somebody that I entrusted.
Miss. FREAME sworn.
Q. Do you ever act in your father's business? - I do.
Q. Was it over the custom in his business for the bread to go out without being counted? - Never.
Q. Might it not happen, by accident, that bread may go out and account given of it afterwards? - Never.
Mr. Knowlys. Is not the bread, when taken out by the journeymen, always taken from the shop? - Always.
The prisoner called one witness to his character.
GUILTY. (Aged 31)
Imprisoned six months in the House of Correction, and fined 1s.
Tried by the second Middlesex Jury before Mr. COMMON SERJEANT.
[trialAccount t17950916-85]
455. WILLIAM BLOCK was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 15th of August, a cloth coat, value 30s. the goods of Angus Cameron.
ANGUS CAMERON sworn.
I live in Beet-street, near Golden-square.
Q. Did you lose a cloth coat at any time? - Yes, from the Bricklayer's Arms, King-street, Golden-square; the taylor had it, and was coming home with it, and he happened to call in the public house; I was in the public house, and he put it down on the seat beside of him; it was in the parlour; he told me that he had got my great coat, and was going home with it; I told him I would take it home myself. The prisoner at the bar was smoaking his pipe in the parlour at the same time; he had used that house for a month before that, and never was out of it. When I was going home I asked the taylor where was the coat; and he put his hand behind him and did not find it, and there was nobody missed out of the room but the prisoner at the bar; we could not hear of him any more that night not the next day. I staid about an hour afterwards, but the coat was missed from about five minutes after he went out of the room; he never returned to the house; I never see him till last Monday week when he was apprehended.
Q. What day were you at the Brick-layers Arms? - Saturday, the 15th of August, about eight o'clock at night. He was taken up in St. Giles's. The people that were in the room knew that he stole the coat.
Q. Was the coat ever found? - No, it was not.
Q. Are any people here that live in that public house? - No. When he was apprehended I told him if he would give me the duplicate of the coat, I would let him go about his business; he told me that he had sold it for four shillings and sixpence.
Q. Did he say where? - He did not.
Q. And he never said he had sold it till you told him it would be better for him to do so? - No.
JOHN AUSTON sworn.
Q. Were you at the public house? - Yes.
Q. Did you see the prisoner take away the coat? - I did not. I have known the house some time.
Q. Had he frequented it any time? -He had.
Q. Do you know whether he ever came to that house after the coat was missing? - I never see him there.
Not GUILTY.
Tried by the second Middlesex Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
[trialAccount t17950916-86]
456. WILLIAM SMITH and WILLIAM TOOKE were indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 15th ofSeptember, ten pair of leather shoes, value 1l. 10s. two cotton patches for gowns, value 1l. eleven silk handkerchiefs, value 1l. 10s. six pair of cotton stocks, value 6s. and three pair of childrens morocco shoes, value 3s. the goods of Susanna Gillam, in her dwelling house.
WILLIAM MILLER sworn.
I drive the Tottenham stage, for Mr. Kimpton; I have drove it near these twenty years; I live at Tottenham. The 15th of this month, Tuesday sevenight, when I came out of my back door, I see a man standing with a bundle under his arm; I says to him, what do you want? he makes answer and says to me, nothing. That was Smith I believe, but I am not certain, because I did not take him, and I did not take particular notice when I was at the gate. I see Mrs. Gillam's gate open and door open; it was the back part of the house; there is a little bit of ground called a yard to it; and I calls out Mrs. Gillam! and nobody answered. I went then to the back door, and as I went to the back door I see two men come out of the shop, and I asked them where Mrs. Gillam was? and they made answer up stairs; one of the two did; but positively I could not tell which in that time, and I says to him, then you shall stop till Mrs. Gillam comes; when they both made for the gate to get away as fast as they could; I catched hold of one, the beggest of the two, Tooke, and I never broke from him, nor he from me till such times as I brought him down and was on him.
Q. You secured him? - Yes, by myself. The other ran away; there was nobody near at all to assist at that time; there were several handkerchiefs on the ground as he lay, as we were both down.
Q. What is Mrs. Gillam? - She keeps a linen draper's shop, nothing else.
Q. Do you mean to say that the handkerchiefs dropped from this man? - Yes; they dropped from him out of the breast part. The constable has them.
Q. Do you know which way these men got in this house? - At the back door.
Q. Could they have got in at the front? - No, it was fastened.
Q. Do you know any thing of the other man that got away? - Not to swear to him.
Q. What time of the day was this? -About a quarter after eight in the morning, as near as can be.
FREDERIC TANSLEY sworn.
I am a shoe-maker, at Tottenham. About eight o'clock in the morning, or a trifle after, Tuesday week, the 15th of this month, I see Smith go by with a bundle under his arm, by my house; I live about six or seven doors from Mrs. Gillam, about seventy yards, not much more. I suspected that he had stole this bundle. As such Miller sent for me to take Tooke in charge, and he informed me that Mrs. Gillam was robbed; and as such I said, I see a man go by with a bundle under his arm, and I did not doubt but he was the person that stole the property; as such I sent a young man, named Wagstaff, after Smith immediately; and he took the man with the property under his arm, against the place which we call the George and Vulture, in Tottenham; as such he brought him back to me immediately, and I searched his pockets, and in his pockets I found five pair of stockings, and three pair of childrens shoes buttoned up in the flap of his breeches. I have got the property now in court.(Produced.)
- WAGSTAFF sworn.
I am a gardener at Tottenham. The constable sent me after this man, Smith, to take him, and I took him at the George and Vulture, at Tottenham, and brought him back, and delivered him to the constable, with the goods under his arm, ten pair of shoes and two gown pieces.(Produced.)
Tanslev. I have had them in my care ever since. I found eleven silk handkerchiefs in the yard between Gillam's house and Miller's.
Q. To Miller. These are the same handkerchiefs you delivered to the constable? - They are.
Mrs. Gillam being a quaker declined taking an oath.
Prisoner Tooke. On Monday, the 14th of this month, I was on my journey from Hertfordshire to London; being late in the evening I got a list as far as Enfield highway; from there I walked to Edmonton, where the Statute was then holden; I went into a booth, where I staid till quite day light; proceeding on towards London I had occasion to retire up a passage, where I had not been above two minutes before a man came in and called out Mrs. Gillam! and the people went out, and I went out, and this man laid hold of me, and said, that I had been in the house. What I stand charged at the bar with I am entirely innocent of, neither do I know any thing at all of the parties that were there. I was coming up to London in hopes of getting a birth on board a man of war.
Prisoner Smith. As I was going on Lower Edmonton, to my friends, going along in the morning I see this bundle laying in the middle of the road, and picked it up; I did not know where it came from; about two or three minutes before that I met two men running up the road. I was bringing of it back, and there were two men came and catched hold of me, when I resigned directly, and they brought me back; I never said a word. I have had my friends waiting here several days, but they are not here now.
Q. To Miller. Is Mrs. Gillam the owner of the house? - She is.
Richard Smith, GUILTY. Death.(Aged 23.)
William Tooke, GUILTY. Death.(Aged 24.)
Tried by the second Middlesex Jury before Mr. COMMON SERJEANT.

457. ANN BONE was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 2d of July, two silk handkerchiefs, value 2s. a man's woollen cloth jacket, value 5s. a man's cotton waistcoat, value 2s. a check shirt, value 2s. and a pair of cotton stockings, value 1s. 6d. the goods of John Brown.
JOHN BROWN sworn.
I am a sailor, a lodger; I was robbed the 5th of July, between the hours of eleven and twelve, I came up from Gravesend; I knocked at the prisoner's door, and the prisoner let me in. I happened to be picked up by her one night about a week or a fortnight before.
Q. Where did she live? - In Thomas's-court, I think the name is. I cannot say I was over and above sober, not I was not drunk; I was sensible of what property I had about me.
Q. Are you a married or a single man? - Single man. Another young fellow and a woman was with her. I took in a handkerchief with a bundle, and whichcontained two silk handkerchiefs, &c. all tied up in a silk handkerchief.
Q. Was there another silk handkerchief besides? - Yes, in the jacket pocket. I laid them down along side of me on the bed, the bed was down.
Q. Did you undress and go to bed? - No; I sat, with the things, along side of her. I sent for a pot of beer and staid there till about one o'clock, and the other parties, they went away; I still had the bundle along side of me for ten minutes after they were gone, and I had occasion to get up to do something for myself, and the prisoner went twice to the door.
Q. Did you go out of the room? - No, I did not.
Q. Was your back turned to her? - Yes. When I came forward to the bed I missed my bundle; I asked her for the bundle; and she said she knew nothing about it. With that she went to go out, and I detained her from going out; when she found that I would not let her go out she seemed rather reconciled, and laid down on the bed. I sat up along side of her till I was rather tired, and I happened to fall back myself and drop asleep; with that the first thing that awaked me was her hand in my pocket, and I had about eight or nine shillings in it altogether; on my stirring with that she drew her hand away from me, and pretended to be asleep; I laid there some time and dropped off again, and she went out, and I did not see any more of her till the morning, she left me in the room. In the morning she came in, and I asked her for my bundle; and she said she knew nothing about it. With that I sent for a constable, and a constable came; the the pawnbroker's, and there he found a waistcoat that she pawned.
WILLIAM MATHER sworn.
I am a constable of St. Luke's; I produce a waistcoat that I found pledged at Mrs. West's, the corner of Fan-street, Aldersgate-street.
Q. Who has kept that waistcoat? - I have.
- sworn.
I live with Mrs. West, in Aldersgate-street.
Q. Of whom did you receive that waistcoat which the constable has? - Of the prisoner at the bar, the 3d of July, in the morning, before ten o'clock; I had seen her before; I supposed that she had a husband that belonged to her. The constable called in the morning and I was not in the way, and he took the waistcoat to the magistrate, and has had it ever since.
Q. To the Constable. Did you examine this woman - Yes.
Q. Did you find any duplicates? - Nothing of the kind.(The waistcoat deposed to.)
Prisoner. He gave me the waistcoat to sleep with me all night. About twelve or one o'clock he came and knocked at my door; there are two or three people more in the lodging house; with that they told us we might go to bed, we should be very safe; and when we awaked in the morning we found the door wide open. It was a common lodging house.
Q. To Prosecutor. On the solemn oath you have taken, did you give her that waistcoat? - I did not.
Q. Did you give her any money? - Only some money to get some beer.
Q. You staid with her all night? - I did, when I found I had lost my bundle.
GUILTY. (Aged 54.)
Imprisoned one year in the House of Correction and fined 1s.
Tried by the London Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
[trialAccount t17950916-88]
458. FRANCES HUGHES was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 5th of September, a cotton handkerchief, value 6d. and eight shillings and sixpence in monies numbered; the goods and monies of Jeremiah Mackay.
JERMIAH MACKAY sworn.
I am a watchman; I went along with this woman, and she and I were in bed together; I fell asleep, and she got up and went away, and left the door open, and I did not miss her till the girl of the house came up and awaked me.
Q. Did you know this woman before? - Yes.
Q. Did you live with her? - No, I did not; she lodged in a place called Church-lane, St. Giles's.
Q. What time of the night did you go to her? - About six o'clock.
Q. Before you went on your duty? - Yes.
Q. What time did she go away? - I cannot say. The girl came up to me at nine o'clock, and asked me where she was? I said, I did not know; the girl asked me if I had any money? I told her I had. I missed eight shillings and sixpence, and my pocket handkerchief; I was sober enough.
Q. You knew where to find this woman the next day? - No, I did not.
Q. You knew where she lodged? - She told me the present time she lodged there; but I did not know where she lodged the next night.
Q. Did you enquire? - No, I did not.
Q. What was you to give her? - She asked nothing at all. I did not tell her about any thing, only went to bed along with her.
Q. You made some bargain? - Only to go along with her. I had been with her once before in the same house. I told the watchman belonging to the place if he see her to take her up; and so he did. I cannot swear whether it was she or the girl that awaked me took it.
Not GUILTY.
Tried by the second Middlesex Jury before Mr. COMMON SERJEANT.
[trialAccount t17950916-89]
459. JOHN SCHROEDER was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 25th of August, a cotton gown, value 2s. the goods of John Smout.
MARGARET SMOUT sworn.
I am the wife of John Smout, housekeepers, at No. 15, Old street, Soho; the prisoner at the bar came and knocked at the door, and the maid opened the door, and he presented this bill; I was not present; she rung the bell; Mr. Cokum came down stairs; I came down while he was in the passage; I was coming up the street when he knocked at the door; I did not hear him knock.
Q. Where did you find the prisoner standing? - Within the passage. I went up and stood at the yard door, with my child in my arms, the yard of the house, and Mr. Cokum came down stairs, and he gave Mr. Cokum this paper; I see him, and Mr. Cokum turned it over, seeing so many names, but he could not make any thing out of it.
Q. Did he give it you? - No, he did not. We found it in the passage, before we took him out of the passage, Mr. Cokum could not understand what he said, he spoke German, he would not speak English; and Mr. Cokum says, come up stairs, I have one of your countrymen in my shop, and he will tell me what you want. With that he went up stairs, and I stood at the yard door, and I heard the shop door shut, and I heardthe foot coming down stairs, and I heard it stop.
Q. Which is the shop door? - It is the top of the house. I heard the foot stop, and I turned from the yard door to go up stairs, and I met him on the last flight of stairs; he had got something under his coat; I said, what have you got there? and he said nothing, but pushed by me, and I stopped him at the parlour and pulled the gown from under his coat.
Q. Where was this gown kept? - It was laying on the cradle; I had it to cover my child in the cradle, in the dining room, in the first floor.
Q. Did he pass that dining room to come out of the shop? - Yes. I have kept it ever since.
Q. You are sure it is your's? - Yes.
Q. When did you find that paper? -After he was taken away, to go to the justice. He got away, and they called out after him and brought him back.
Prisoner. I went there to enquire for Mr. Smout, who is a Sweedish gentleman. Ask her whether the gown was not on the same premises when I was brought back again? - Yes, because at our parlour door I pulled the property from under his coat, and then he got out of the street door.
Prisoner. When I came down stairs the gown was on the banisters; she said so before the justice? - No, it was in the cradle.
Court. You did not see him come out of the dining room? - I did not.
JAMES KENNEDY sworn.
I am an officer belonging to Marlborough-street. On the 25th of August, there was a call of stop thief; in the street; the prosecutor lives just by my house; I went to the street and took the prisoner into custody; the mob was about him at the time.
Prisoner. That gentleman did not take me, a shoe-maker took me.
Court to Kennedy. He was delivered up to you, I suppose? - Yes.
Q. How far was this from the house of the lady? - A hundred yards, not much further. I took him strait to the office, and I told her to follow me to Marlborough-street office; she was among the people.
Q. What did she charge him with? - With taking a gown.
Q. Did the shew you a gown at that time? - No. In searching him I found these fourteen petticoats, and every one of them is to a different trade, signed all by a surgeon.
Prisoner. When I came to this gentlewoman's house, I was recommended by a German, with a letter; I went up stairs, coming down stairs they gave me an answer that I was to call again the next day. There was a gown on the banisters, and when I came down stairs I never touched the gown at all; I am as innocent as a child unborn; I came out and went out of doors, and the lady called out stop him! and a shoe-maker stopped me; the said Mr. Kennedy did not make his appearance at all, he was only the officer in Marlborough-street. When we did come there I was committed to New Prison, Clerkenwell; I was there from Tuesday till Saturday, and then I was discharged from the justice; the lady did not make her appearance, and then I was remanded back to New Prison for three weeks; then on Saturday they found a bill against me, which I am as innocent of as a child unborn. I was quite entirely discharged from the justice.
Court to Prosecutrix. Was this man examined once or twice? - He was only examined once before me; he was to be brought up the next day on account of a watch that he had on him; theythought to find out to whom it belonged.
Prisoner. Mr. Kirby knows very well. I have a wife and three small children, and have lost one of my eyes.
Court to Prosecturix. Was he brought back to the house? - Yes, the mob brought him back, and then as we were going from the house again Mr. Kennedy met him.
GUILTY. (Aged 26)
Transported for seven years.
Tried by the second Middlesex Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
[trialAccount t17950916-90]
460. ELIZABETH TRIMBETH was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 22d of August, a silk purse, value 3d. six guineas, and eighteen shillings; the goods and monies of Amantero Sieys.
AN INTERPRETER sworn.
AMANTERO SIEYS sworn.
I am from a place called Masita, near Marseilles; I am a sailor. After my supper, on the 22d of August, at half past nine, I went to a public-house, the corner of Well-street, Ratcliff-highway; there were three of us together; we were drinking a pot of beer; the other two went out and left me in the public house; I drew out my purse and took out a sixpence, to pay for the pot of beer which I had; the landlord returned me the change, and at the same time I went out; and this young woman was in the room when I paid the landlord; when I went out the girl followed me, she followed me into the middle of the street, passed the side of me and drew the purse from my pocket; she instantly ran across the road, and I could not find her; she ran into some house; it was only two or three steps to run across. I remained in the middle of the road frightened, the shortest thing that I thought was to go and advise with the landlord where I was drinking. Then I went about to all the houses, and there enquired after her, and could not find her. The publican asked me where I lived? I told him at Mr. Bush's; we went to Mr. Bush's, and the publican, and Mr. Bush and me went in pursuit of this girl; after they were in the road they desired me to go back and leave them together; that if in case the girl should perceive me, she might have some suspicion; and I returned to the public house; Mr. Bush and the publican went in search of this girl in different places till they found her.
Q. Did you ever find your money? - It is impossible to find it.
Q. Are you sure that is the young woman? - Yes. I felt her hand in my pocket. It contained six guineas in gold, and eighteen shillings in silver.
JOSEPH BARE sworn.
I keep the Marquis of Cornwallis public house, the corner of Well-street. On Saturday night, between nine and ten, this man came into my house and called for a pot of beer; about ten o'clock he went away; this girl was going out with him; he paid me for a pot of beer out of his purse, and the girl stood close by with him; I see them go out together. A short time after the poor man came in all of a frustration; he could not speak any English, and I could not rightly understand what was the matter with him, till he shewed me that somebody had taken something out of his pocket; I could just make out a few words, that he lodged at Mr. Bush's; I went with him to Mr. Bush's, and I asked Mr. Bush to explain what it was that man meant.
Q. What is the prisoner? - I believe she is a girl of the town; she had come in for a pot of ale, but by some means or other she got into acquaintance with this man; they went out in company together posit vely. Then Mr. Bush told me how he was robbed; I went immediately in pursuit along with Mr. Bush, and between eleven and twelve I apprehended the prisoner, the same night; I see her have all these new things in her possession, and a quantity of clothes, which I thought fit to take from her. I found a number of gold rings, which she said she had been purchasing that night. There were eighteen shillings found on her, and six shillings and sixpence that she said she had given to some girl that was along with her. Here is a new pair of shoes, stockings, garters, gown, cap, new shift, and petticoat.
Q. Did she tell you where she bought all these things? - She was so much in liquor that I could hardly make out what she said. The folks of the house said that she had lost half a guinea out of her hand just before, but which I could not find.
Q. Was she in liquor when she left your house? - No.
Q. How do you know she purchased these things that night? - She had no rings on her fingers when she went out of my house, because she paid me for a pot of ale that night. Here are the gold bobs, the ears had been bored that night.
Prisoner. Mr. Bare sent a woman to pull the ear rings out of my ears, and she made my ears bleed. Mr. Bush will be upon his oath that that man had but eighteen pence when he came out. I went in first into Mr. Bare's house, I went in for a pot of ale, for a boatswain of a ship that was at our house; with that this old gentleman was there; he had been there drinking all day, and he spoke to me, and I went out with him, and there were two girls at the door, and he stopped talking to them, About two hours after I was drinking in a public house, and Mr. Bare came and took me over to his house, and searched me all over, and took a locket off my neck, and put me in the watch-house, and on Monday he came and put me in Newgate, and this gentleman came and said that if I would make up the money that I had from him, he would free me of it; I said I had nothing of his; and he gave me some little trisle that he had in his pocket, and bid me good by.
JOHN BABTIST BUSH sworn.
This man lodged with me a fortnight, and the very next day after he paid me a guinea and a half, and I gave him eighteen-pence in change, and he said that was all he had in the world, and the next day he came and borrowed some money to buy some tobacco, and I lent it him, and afterwards he came to me about ten o'clock, and told me that he had been robbed of some money; Mr. Bare asked me to be so kind as to go with him, and he would go and seek for the girl; I went with Mr. Bare round Rosemary lane, and he found the girl at a public house, and we took her, I did not know the girl, the prosecutor says, that is the girl.
Q. When did he tell you he had no money? - On the 13th of August, he paid me the guinea and a half, and told me that he had no more money than that, and I gave him eighteen pence change.
Court to Bare. When he pulled out his purse what money had he in it? - I did not see what he had in it, but since the 13th I have changed one or two guineas at my house; I think there was some money in the purse.
Q. To Sieys. How came you to tell Mr. Bush that you had no more money? - I did not say so
Q. What did you pay on the 14th of August? - A guinea and a half.
Q. How much money had you on the 14th of August? - Nine guineas and a half.
ELIZABETH CONNER sworn.
I am a grocer and cheesemonger; the prisoner's parents are honest just people; I have known her for four years, she has been in and out of my house, and I have not known her guilty of the least injustice in my life; her parents to my knowledge maintains her.
Q. She is not on the town at all? - Not to my knowledge, her father lives in Stepney Causeway, the number of the house I cannot tell, but it is a remarkable white house, let out in tenements.
Q. How far is that from Well-street? - I cannot pretend to say.
Bare. About a mile.
Conner. She is a very sober young woman, I know I never see her disguised upon my oath.
Q. What age is she? - I cannot pretend to say; her father is a labouring man, he has foremerly been a seafaring man, and he works in some ship now, and is an honest just man.
ANN NICHOLS sworn.
I am a sailor's wife, I live in East Smithfield; I have known the prisoner not quite three months, she is a poor misfortunate girl, and did what she could for her living; this young woman has been very honest since I knew her, she lives in Vinegar-yard, just by Well-street.
Q. You never heard whether she had in the last three months lived at Stepney Causeway? - Her father and mother lived there.
Q. She then lived with her father and mother? - No, she did not.
Q. To Prosecutor. When you came out of the public house, did you speak to any other woman at all? - I spoke to me one at all, there was nobody there but this one person.
Q. To Bare. Did you happen to see whether there were any other women at the door? - I did not see any other, I was not out of doors directly.
GUILTY. (Aged 17.)
Imprisoned three months in Newgate and and fined 1s.
Tried by the second Middlesex Jury before Mr. COMMON SERJEANT.
[trialAccount t17950916-91]
461. THOMAS LEGGE was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 4th of September, fourteen pounds weight of lead, value 18d. belonging to Richard Jefferies, affixed to a certain dwelling of his.
RICHARD JEFFERIES sworn.
I am a carpenter. On Friday, the 4th of this month, the lead was lost from a house that I was going to raise the story.
Q. You have the lease of it? - Yes. About five o'clock I met the prisoner with the lead, coming out of the premises, wrapped up in a flannel jacket, under his arm, I see him coming out, I waited till he came up to me, and asked him what he had got under his arm? he made answer, nothing belonging to you sir? On that I insisted on his turning his waistcoat aside, and I see it was lead, with that I said, you rascal, you have been robbing me of lead; with that I attempted to get it from under his arm, he tried to prevent it, but I got it from him, and I found it was the hopper head of a trunk; I got a carpenter's chissel to open it, is that which first of all receives the water from the gutter of the roof, to carry it to the pipe, it is like a square bason; I see it inthe morning, I suppose about six o'clock.
Q. Was it fixed there safe? - Yes, it was fixed to the wall by a couple of wall hooks.
Q. Did it appear to you that the lead was recently taken? - Yes, by the wall hooks, which were drawn out, they were thrown away, there was nothing to cut, he took it compleatly, because it is nothing to cut.
Q. Did you examine the place from whence it was taken? - I did, the same day, and the two holes corresponded exactly with the two holes where the hold fasts were.
Q. Have you any doubt it is your's? - None, perfectly satisfied. When I took the prisoner, instead of acknowledging his fault, he upt with his fist and knocked me down, and I believe he would have murdered me had it been in his power; he did not compleatly knock me down, but he knocked me as far as your lordship, and then I went after him and tripped up his heels, and he got up again, and I knocked him down three times.
Q. Did he strike you with his fist first? - Yes, he did, after I had got the lead from him he struck me a blow just on the side of my temple. He was a task master bricklayer, and I had employed him at that time to do some work by the rod.
Mr. Jackson. Was this person in your employ? - I had engaged him to do some brick work for me; he had not done any thing.
Q. In whose employ may you be? - In my own.
Q. What other descriptions of trades might be employed in repairing of this house? - Carpenters.
Q. No plumbers? - No.
Q. How many carpenters might be employed in the house? - Two.
Q. How many bricklayers? - Not one.
Q. I think you said that you was raising the roofs of these house? - Yes.
Q. How long might they have been your houses? - About two years.
Q. Will you have the goodness to describe the contract between Mr. Wright and you, respecting these houses? - I made an agreement to sell him the two houses when the attick story was carried up; there was a specific sum agreed upon when this atrick story was carried up.
Q. Who was to pay the bricklayer? - Me.
Q. Had you happened to receive any earnest money of Mr. Wright? - Not a shilling.
Q. Was your agreement in writing? - Not a word of it.
Q. You have been always good friends with this man up to this time? - Yes.
Q. Does there happen to be any account existing between him and you? - He had done a garden wall for me.
Q. When this happened were you in his debt? - No.
Q. Had he been impertinent enough to tell you that you owed him five pounds? - I never paid him five pounds in my life, the work that he did for me had not amounted but to thirty shillings in the whole, and I gave him a guinea and a half, and he left the work.
Q. Did he ever ask you for any further sum that you owed him? - No.
Q. I think you have described a pretty good pitched battle at this taking, you did not strike the first blow? - No.
Q. You did not d-n his eyes for a rascal and a thief? - I told him he was a scoundrel and a thief.
Q. What progress had you made in taking off the roof? - The rafters were taken down, and the top most likely uncovered.
Q. The lead had been all taken off of course? - That was taken away by theplumbers two or three days before.
Q. In the course of clearing the lead from different houses, have you ever employed the prisoner for that purpose? - Yes, one particular time I did, but that was another house, about a twelve month ago, and I forgot the lead, and I asked him to bring it down from the garret.
Q. I think by your own account you knocked him down three times; did you happen to continue in possession of his person; pray did you let him go home? - He went away after I took the lead from him; I went to get the patrols to take him up; I did detain him a considerable time, but afterwards I left him in the care of two carpenters while I went for the patrol, and he got away from them, and I found him at home, laying on the bed, and his wife denied him.
Q. You did not say that you would make him repent of this? - I did not.
Q. Nor nothing of that kind? - No.
Q. Did he happen to say to you, I will go with you where you please? - No, not a single syllable of that sort.
Q. Did he not say after you had struck him, you are a bad man, did not I tell you where I found it, and I was coming with it to you? - No.
Q. Has it ever happened to you to be in any embarrassment about lead? - No.
Q. Never called upon by any legal process whatever, to answer for a mistake of your own in having taken lead? - Never.
Q. Do you happen to know that the prisoner at the bar was ever subpoenaed against you, to give evidence against you? - Yes.
Q. Then in point of fact something like a legal process has issued against you for mistaking property in lead? - No, it was my own property. When I built this house, the house came to fifteen hundred pounds, it was built according to contract; there was a man that I employed in the building gave wrong dimensions to the plumber; when the lead was brought there, and laid down in this roof, one Mr. Dixon said, this is not the exact lead that is in my agreement; I was under the necessity of taking that lead up and get fresh lead, but there was some that I put into the gutters and other parts, and there was a certain quantity left behind, and there was an action about the extras for two hundred and fifty pounds; Mr. Dixon said there was lead left in the house, and that I had not allowed for this lead that I had taken away from his house; it was an action that I brought against him.
Q. In that case, whatever the description of it was, he was subpoenaed against you? - He was, but he never came forward.
Q. Since then there has been some little interruption in your friendship? - Never, or else I would not have done all I could to recommend him.
Q. You have stated that other persons were employed in the house besides this man, might they not have taken the lead from the part to which it was supposed to be detached, as well as himself? - They might have done so.
Q. Had the lead of the gutters been taken down? - Certainly, and locked up, this had no connection whatever with him.
Q. And this hopper remained with two nails? - It has no other support, it has no support from the gutter.
Q. Pray did Mr. Wright come in and out pretty often? - He did, sometimes.
Q. Who happens to have the key? - There was no key on the door, the workmen were at work at five o'clock.
Q. In the course of the visits of Mr. Wright, he gave directions how he would have things go on? - He was to receive no orders but from me; I don't think at this time that Mr. Wright had been there at all. I pointed out the way in whichI meant to do it, and he was satisfied.
Prisoner. On the 1st of September I erected a scaffold at the front of these two houses; in the evening Mr. Wright's carpenter took the lead down from these buildings; on the 4th of September I went to the building to see for Mr. Jefferies, for some materials to mix up the lime, to get on with the brick work, and I did not see Mr. Jefferies there; I went down into the lower part of the house, and I found this property among the rubbish, and the carpenters were at work on the premises at the same time; it was either ten minutes before five, or after, I am not certain which.
Court to Prosecutor. What time did you go to your house that night? - About five o'clock.
Q. What time did you go to the prisoner's house? - It might be in the course of two hours.
Mr. Jackson. It was not ten o'clock? - No, I fancy he was in St. Martin's round-house by that time.
The prisoner called seven witnesses who gave him a good character.
GUILTY. (Aged 24.)
Transported for seven years.
Tried by the second Middlesex Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
[trialAccount t17950916-92]
462. MARY MOORE was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 10th of September, nine yards of printed cotton, value 12s the goods of Robert MacGlew, privately on his shop.
ROBERT MAC GLEW sworn.
I live in Bishopsgate-street, a linen draper; the prisoner at the bar came into my shop on Thursday, the 10th of September, a little after eight in the evening, and desired to look at some printed callico for a gown, I shewed her several, she did not fix on any of them, she desired me to shew her some at all wide; I shewed her several of them, she did not fix on any of them; I then went round to the cottons, and I observed when I came back again the prisoner's gown in a very awkward manner, as if something were under it; I shewed her several more, but she did not fix on any of them, she liked one pretty well, she said she would determine of it that night, and come the next morning for it; I was taking the prints that I had shewn, to see if there were any which she might like, which she had seen; when I was doing that she turned on her heel and wished me a good night, and went out; I then jumped over the counter, and desired the shopman to run after her, and stop her immediately, which he did, he overtook her about a dozen yards from the door; I followed him likewise myself, and began to feel about for the gown, where I thought the bundle was, then it was gone; the shopman that laid hold of her first, here it lays, I I see him pick it up, I brought her in and had her examined.
Prisoner. Was there not another man in the shop, looking at goods the same time? - There was, the man was a neighbour.
Q. What is your man's name? - Atkinson, he is not here, the boy is, I could not possibly bring them both; Atkinson was at the opposite counter.
JAMES DAY sworn.
Q. How old are you? - Seventeen. I am servant to Robert Mac Glew. On Thursday night between eight and nine o'clock the 10th of September last; Mary Moore came into the shop and asked to look at some printed callicoes for a gown; some time after she went out; I see mymaster shew her a great many goods, and when she went out he told the shopman to stop her, and I immediately went to the door, and we were about a dozen yards from the door, and I see her drop this piece of printed callico, and I see the young man stop her, and he picked it up. There were no other customers in the shop.
Q. Where was Atkinson? - Behind the opposite counter.
- SAPWELL sworn.
I produce the cotton, I received it of the prosecutor.
Prosecutor. I am sure it is mine, there are several marks, it has been in the house these six years almost
Prisoner. I am quite innocent, I have nothing more to say, I hope you will consider that my friends are all in the country, and I have got nobody here to speak for me.
GUILTY,
Of stealing, but not privately in the shop. (Aged 21.)
Imprisoned three months in Newgate and fined 1s.
Tried by the London Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
